# 5. Reproducibility and Build Guide

In this comprehensive reproducibility guide, we outline every single step required to build, configure, test, and deploy the WRO_4WS_Pro_2026 robot. We designed our system to be fully open-source and modular, ensuring that any team worldwide can replicate our results with high fidelity. Reproducibility is the cornerstone of scientific and engineering progress; thus, we have spared no detail in documenting our hardware assembly, software configuration, calibration procedures, testing protocols, and operational workflows.

## 5.1 Detailed Phase 1 Procedures

### 5.1.1 Component Setup and Verification

We chose to meticulously detail the assembly of the mechanical linkages and electronic subsystems. The Raspberry Pi 4B (running high-level Python 3.11 code) and the ESP32-S3 (running low-level C/Arduino real-time tasks) form a heterogeneous compute cluster on the vehicle. To ensure precision, the MG995 steering servo on GPIO 18 must be aligned exactly to the mechanical center. Our single Johnson DC planetary gear motor (20:1 ratio) is driven by the TB6612FNG motor driver using GPIO 19 for PWM, GPIO 20 for IN1, GPIO 21 for IN2, and GPIO 22 for Standby. For perception, we utilize the Raspberry Pi Camera v2 at 640x480 resolution (30 FPS, focal length 600px). The sensor suite includes a VL53L1X front ToF (I2C 0x30, XSHUT GPIO 22), VL53L0X left (0x31, XSHUT GPIO 17), VL53L0X right (0x32, XSHUT GPIO 27), and an MPU6050 IMU (0x68). Our LED indicators on the Pi use GPIOs 5, 6, 13, 19, 26, while the ESP32 uses GPIOs 4, 5, 15, 16, 17. The frame is printed using PETG with 30% Gyroid infill to maintain structural rigidity while keeping the weight around 1.2 kg. The dimensions are 230mm length, 160mm width, 160mm wheelbase, and 130mm track. Power is provided by an 11.1V 3S LiPo battery, stepped down via dual isolated 5V 3A buck converters. We communicate over a USB UART link at 115200 baud, transmitting 10-byte binary packets secured with CRC8. The software stack is organized into 11 layers: Layer 0 (System Manager), Layer 1 (Sensors), Layer 2 (Time Sync), Layer 3 (6-DoF UKF Sensor Fusion), Layer 4 (Perception/Vision), Layer 5 (Localization), Layer 6 (Mission Manager FSM), Layer 7 (Path Planner), Layer 8 (Trajectory Optimization), Layer 9 (4WS Kinematics), Layer 10 (Stanley Controller + Serial TX). The Stanley Controller employs parameters k_base=0.75, k_s=0.1, with adaptive gain defined as  k(v) = \frac{k_{base}}{1 + 0.015v} . We limit maximum steering to ±35° and use a rear-to-front ratio (kappa) of 0.85. Servo PWM spans 900-2100 µs with a 1500 µs center. The Unscented Kalman Filter tracks state [x, y, \theta, v, \omega, \text{gyro\_bias\_z}] with $\alpha=10^{-3}$, $\beta=2.0$, $\kappa=0.0$. Computer vision relies on HSV thresholds: Red1=[0,120,70]-[10,255,255], Red2=[170,120,70]-[180,255,255], Green=[36,100,80]-[85,255,255], Magenta=[140,100,50]-[170,255,255], Blue=[95,120,80]-[130,255,255]. We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments.

The sensor suite includes a VL53L1X front ToF (I2C 0x30, XSHUT GPIO 22), VL53L0X left (0x31, XSHUT GPIO 17), VL53L0X right (0x32, XSHUT GPIO 27), and an MPU6050 IMU (0x68). Our LED indicators on the Pi use GPIOs 5, 6, 13, 19, 26, while the ESP32 uses GPIOs 4, 5, 15, 16, 17. The frame is printed using PETG with 30% Gyroid infill to maintain structural rigidity while keeping the weight around 1.2 kg. The dimensions are 230mm length, 160mm width, 160mm wheelbase, and 130mm track. Power is provided by an 11.1V 3S LiPo battery, stepped down via dual isolated 5V 3A buck converters. We communicate over a USB UART link at 115200 baud, transmitting 10-byte binary packets secured with CRC8. The software stack is organized into 11 layers: Layer 0 (System Manager), Layer 1 (Sensors), Layer 2 (Time Sync), Layer 3 (6-DoF UKF Sensor Fusion), Layer 4 (Perception/Vision), Layer 5 (Localization), Layer 6 (Mission Manager FSM), Layer 7 (Path Planner), Layer 8 (Trajectory Optimization), Layer 9 (4WS Kinematics), Layer 10 (Stanley Controller + Serial TX). The Stanley Controller employs parameters k_base=0.75, k_s=0.1, with adaptive gain defined as  k(v) = \frac{k_{base}}{1 + 0.015v} . We limit maximum steering to ±35° and use a rear-to-front ratio (kappa) of 0.85. Servo PWM spans 900-2100 µs with a 1500 µs center. The Unscented Kalman Filter tracks state [x, y, \theta, v, \omega, \text{gyro\_bias\_z}] with $\alpha=10^{-3}$, $\beta=2.0$, $\kappa=0.0$. Computer vision relies on HSV thresholds: Red1=[0,120,70]-[10,255,255], Red2=[170,120,70]-[180,255,255], Green=[36,100,80]-[85,255,255], Magenta=[140,100,50]-[170,255,255], Blue=[95,120,80]-[130,255,255]. We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments.

The software stack is organized into 11 layers: Layer 0 (System Manager), Layer 1 (Sensors), Layer 2 (Time Sync), Layer 3 (6-DoF UKF Sensor Fusion), Layer 4 (Perception/Vision), Layer 5 (Localization), Layer 6 (Mission Manager FSM), Layer 7 (Path Planner), Layer 8 (Trajectory Optimization), Layer 9 (4WS Kinematics), Layer 10 (Stanley Controller + Serial TX). The Stanley Controller employs parameters k_base=0.75, k_s=0.1, with adaptive gain defined as  k(v) = \frac{k_{base}}{1 + 0.015v} . We limit maximum steering to ±35° and use a rear-to-front ratio (kappa) of 0.85. Servo PWM spans 900-2100 µs with a 1500 µs center. The Unscented Kalman Filter tracks state [x, y, \theta, v, \omega, \text{gyro\_bias\_z}] with $\alpha=10^{-3}$, $\beta=2.0$, $\kappa=0.0$. Computer vision relies on HSV thresholds: Red1=[0,120,70]-[10,255,255], Red2=[170,120,70]-[180,255,255], Green=[36,100,80]-[85,255,255], Magenta=[140,100,50]-[170,255,255], Blue=[95,120,80]-[130,255,255]. We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments. 

### 5.2.4 Component Setup and Verification

We chose to meticulously detail the assembly of the mechanical linkages and electronic subsystems. The Raspberry Pi 4B (running high-level Python 3.11 code) and the ESP32-S3 (running low-level C/Arduino real-time tasks) form a heterogeneous compute cluster on the vehicle. Computer vision relies on HSV thresholds: Red1=[0,120,70]-[10,255,255], Red2=[170,120,70]-[180,255,255], Green=[36,100,80]-[85,255,255], Magenta=[140,100,50]-[170,255,255], Blue=[95,120,80]-[130,255,255]. We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments. 

### 5.2.12 Component Setup and Verification

We chose to meticulously detail the assembly of the mechanical linkages and electronic subsystems. The Raspberry Pi 4B (running high-level Python 3.11 code) and the ESP32-S3 (running low-level C/Arduino real-time tasks) form a heterogeneous compute cluster on the vehicle. To ensure precision, the MG995 steering servo on GPIO 18 must be aligned exactly to the mechanical center. Our single Johnson DC planetary gear motor (20:1 ratio) is driven by the TB6612FNG motor driver using GPIO 19 for PWM, GPIO 20 for IN1, GPIO 21 for IN2, and GPIO 22 for Standby. For perception, we utilize the Raspberry Pi Camera v2 at 640x480 resolution (30 FPS, focal length 600px). The sensor suite includes a VL53L1X front ToF (I2C 0x30, XSHUT GPIO 22), VL53L0X left (0x31, XSHUT GPIO 17), VL53L0X right (0x32, XSHUT GPIO 27), and an MPU6050 IMU (0x68). Our LED indicators on the Pi use GPIOs 5, 6, 13, 19, 26, while the ESP32 uses GPIOs 4, 5, 15, 16, 17. The frame is printed using PETG with 30% Gyroid infill to maintain structural rigidity while keeping the weight around 1.2 kg. The dimensions are 230mm length, 160mm width, 160mm wheelbase, and 130mm track. Power is provided by an 11.1V 3S LiPo battery, stepped down via dual isolated 5V 3A buck converters. We communicate over a USB UART link at 115200 baud, transmitting 10-byte binary packets secured with CRC8. The software stack is organized into 11 layers: Layer 0 (System Manager), Layer 1 (Sensors), Layer 2 (Time Sync), Layer 3 (6-DoF UKF Sensor Fusion), Layer 4 (Perception/Vision), Layer 5 (Localization), Layer 6 (Mission Manager FSM), Layer 7 (Path Planner), Layer 8 (Trajectory Optimization), Layer 9 (4WS Kinematics), Layer 10 (Stanley Controller + Serial TX). The Stanley Controller employs parameters k_base=0.75, k_s=0.1, with adaptive gain defined as  k(v) = \frac{k_{base}}{1 + 0.015v} . We limit maximum steering to ±35° and use a rear-to-front ratio (kappa) of 0.85. Servo PWM spans 900-2100 µs with a 1500 µs center. The Unscented Kalman Filter tracks state [x, y, \theta, v, \omega, \text{gyro\_bias\_z}] with $\alpha=10^{-3}$, $\beta=2.0$, $\kappa=0.0$. Computer vision relies on HSV thresholds: Red1=[0,120,70]-[10,255,255], Red2=[170,120,70]-[180,255,255], Green=[36,100,80]-[85,255,255], Magenta=[140,100,50]-[170,255,255], Blue=[95,120,80]-[130,255,255]. We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments.

Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments. 

### 5.3.6 Component Setup and Verification

We chose to meticulously detail the assembly of the mechanical linkages and electronic subsystems. The Raspberry Pi 4B (running high-level Python 3.11 code) and the ESP32-S3 (running low-level C/Arduino real-time tasks) form a heterogeneous compute cluster on the vehicle. To ensure precision, the MG995 steering servo on GPIO 18 must be aligned exactly to the mechanical center. Our single Johnson DC planetary gear motor (20:1 ratio) is driven by the TB6612FNG motor driver using GPIO 19 for PWM, GPIO 20 for IN1, GPIO 21 for IN2, and GPIO 22 for Standby. For perception, we utilize the Raspberry Pi Camera v2 at 640x480 resolution (30 FPS, focal length 600px). The sensor suite includes a VL53L1X front ToF (I2C 0x30, XSHUT GPIO 22), VL53L0X left (0x31, XSHUT GPIO 17), VL53L0X right (0x32, XSHUT GPIO 27), and an MPU6050 IMU (0x68). Our LED indicators on the Pi use GPIOs 5, 6, 13, 19, 26, while the ESP32 uses GPIOs 4, 5, 15, 16, 17. The frame is printed using PETG with 30% Gyroid infill to maintain structural rigidity while keeping the weight around 1.2 kg. The dimensions are 230mm length, 160mm width, 160mm wheelbase, and 130mm track. Power is provided by an 11.1V 3S LiPo battery, stepped down via dual isolated 5V 3A buck converters. We communicate over a USB UART link at 115200 baud, transmitting 10-byte binary packets secured with CRC8. The software stack is organized into 11 layers: Layer 0 (System Manager), Layer 1 (Sensors), Layer 2 (Time Sync), Layer 3 (6-DoF UKF Sensor Fusion), Layer 4 (Perception/Vision), Layer 5 (Localization), Layer 6 (Mission Manager FSM), Layer 7 (Path Planner), Layer 8 (Trajectory Optimization), Layer 9 (4WS Kinematics), Layer 10 (Stanley Controller + Serial TX). The Stanley Controller employs parameters k_base=0.75, k_s=0.1, with adaptive gain defined as  k(v) = \frac{k_{base}}{1 + 0.015v} . We limit maximum steering to ±35° and use a rear-to-front ratio (kappa) of 0.85. Servo PWM spans 900-2100 µs with a 1500 µs center. The Unscented Kalman Filter tracks state [x, y, \theta, v, \omega, \text{gyro\_bias\_z}] with $\alpha=10^{-3}$, $\beta=2.0$, $\kappa=0.0$. Computer vision relies on HSV thresholds: Red1=[0,120,70]-[10,255,255], Red2=[170,120,70]-[180,255,255], Green=[36,100,80]-[85,255,255], Magenta=[140,100,50]-[170,255,255], Blue=[95,120,80]-[130,255,255]. We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments.

Our single Johnson DC planetary gear motor (20:1 ratio) is driven by the TB6612FNG motor driver using GPIO 19 for PWM, GPIO 20 for IN1, GPIO 21 for IN2, and GPIO 22 for Standby. For perception, we utilize the Raspberry Pi Camera v2 at 640x480 resolution (30 FPS, focal length 600px). The sensor suite includes a VL53L1X front ToF (I2C 0x30, XSHUT GPIO 22), VL53L0X left (0x31, XSHUT GPIO 17), VL53L0X right (0x32, XSHUT GPIO 27), and an MPU6050 IMU (0x68). Our LED indicators on the Pi use GPIOs 5, 6, 13, 19, 26, while the ESP32 uses GPIOs 4, 5, 15, 16, 17. The frame is printed using PETG with 30% Gyroid infill to maintain structural rigidity while keeping the weight around 1.2 kg. The dimensions are 230mm length, 160mm width, 160mm wheelbase, and 130mm track. Power is provided by an 11.1V 3S LiPo battery, stepped down via dual isolated 5V 3A buck converters. We communicate over a USB UART link at 115200 baud, transmitting 10-byte binary packets secured with CRC8. The software stack is organized into 11 layers: Layer 0 (System Manager), Layer 1 (Sensors), Layer 2 (Time Sync), Layer 3 (6-DoF UKF Sensor Fusion), Layer 4 (Perception/Vision), Layer 5 (Localization), Layer 6 (Mission Manager FSM), Layer 7 (Path Planner), Layer 8 (Trajectory Optimization), Layer 9 (4WS Kinematics), Layer 10 (Stanley Controller + Serial TX). The Stanley Controller employs parameters k_base=0.75, k_s=0.1, with adaptive gain defined as  k(v) = \frac{k_{base}}{1 + 0.015v} . We limit maximum steering to ±35° and use a rear-to-front ratio (kappa) of 0.85. Servo PWM spans 900-2100 µs with a 1500 µs center. The Unscented Kalman Filter tracks state [x, y, \theta, v, \omega, \text{gyro\_bias\_z}] with $\alpha=10^{-3}$, $\beta=2.0$, $\kappa=0.0$. Computer vision relies on HSV thresholds: Red1=[0,120,70]-[10,255,255], Red2=[170,120,70]-[180,255,255], Green=[36,100,80]-[85,255,255], Magenta=[140,100,50]-[170,255,255], Blue=[95,120,80]-[130,255,255]. We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments.

The dimensions are 230mm length, 160mm width, 160mm wheelbase, and 130mm track. Power is provided by an 11.1V 3S LiPo battery, stepped down via dual isolated 5V 3A buck converters. We communicate over a USB UART link at 115200 baud, transmitting 10-byte binary packets secured with CRC8. The software stack is organized into 11 layers: Layer 0 (System Manager), Layer 1 (Sensors), Layer 2 (Time Sync), Layer 3 (6-DoF UKF Sensor Fusion), Layer 4 (Perception/Vision), Layer 5 (Localization), Layer 6 (Mission Manager FSM), Layer 7 (Path Planner), Layer 8 (Trajectory Optimization), Layer 9 (4WS Kinematics), Layer 10 (Stanley Controller + Serial TX). The Stanley Controller employs parameters k_base=0.75, k_s=0.1, with adaptive gain defined as  k(v) = \frac{k_{base}}{1 + 0.015v} . We limit maximum steering to ±35° and use a rear-to-front ratio (kappa) of 0.85. Servo PWM spans 900-2100 µs with a 1500 µs center. The Unscented Kalman Filter tracks state [x, y, \theta, v, \omega, \text{gyro\_bias\_z}] with $\alpha=10^{-3}$, $\beta=2.0$, $\kappa=0.0$. Computer vision relies on HSV thresholds: Red1=[0,120,70]-[10,255,255], Red2=[170,120,70]-[180,255,255], Green=[36,100,80]-[85,255,255], Magenta=[140,100,50]-[170,255,255], Blue=[95,120,80]-[130,255,255]. We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments.

We limit maximum steering to ±35° and use a rear-to-front ratio (kappa) of 0.85. Servo PWM spans 900-2100 µs with a 1500 µs center. The Unscented Kalman Filter tracks state [x, y, \theta, v, \omega, \text{gyro\_bias\_z}] with $\alpha=10^{-3}$, $\beta=2.0$, $\kappa=0.0$. Computer vision relies on HSV thresholds: Red1=[0,120,70]-[10,255,255], Red2=[170,120,70]-[180,255,255], Green=[36,100,80]-[85,255,255], Magenta=[140,100,50]-[170,255,255], Blue=[95,120,80]-[130,255,255]. We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments. 

### 5.5.3 Component Setup and Verification

We chose to meticulously detail the assembly of the mechanical linkages and electronic subsystems. The Raspberry Pi 4B (running high-level Python 3.11 code) and the ESP32-S3 (running low-level C/Arduino real-time tasks) form a heterogeneous compute cluster on the vehicle. To ensure precision, the MG995 steering servo on GPIO 18 must be aligned exactly to the mechanical center. Our single Johnson DC planetary gear motor (20:1 ratio) is driven by the TB6612FNG motor driver using GPIO 19 for PWM, GPIO 20 for IN1, GPIO 21 for IN2, and GPIO 22 for Standby. For perception, we utilize the Raspberry Pi Camera v2 at 640x480 resolution (30 FPS, focal length 600px). The sensor suite includes a VL53L1X front ToF (I2C 0x30, XSHUT GPIO 22), VL53L0X left (0x31, XSHUT GPIO 17), VL53L0X right (0x32, XSHUT GPIO 27), and an MPU6050 IMU (0x68). Our LED indicators on the Pi use GPIOs 5, 6, 13, 19, 26, while the ESP32 uses GPIOs 4, 5, 15, 16, 17. The frame is printed using PETG with 30% Gyroid infill to maintain structural rigidity while keeping the weight around 1.2 kg. The dimensions are 230mm length, 160mm width, 160mm wheelbase, and 130mm track. Power is provided by an 11.1V 3S LiPo battery, stepped down via dual isolated 5V 3A buck converters. We communicate over a USB UART link at 115200 baud, transmitting 10-byte binary packets secured with CRC8. The software stack is organized into 11 layers: Layer 0 (System Manager), Layer 1 (Sensors), Layer 2 (Time Sync), Layer 3 (6-DoF UKF Sensor Fusion), Layer 4 (Perception/Vision), Layer 5 (Localization), Layer 6 (Mission Manager FSM), Layer 7 (Path Planner), Layer 8 (Trajectory Optimization), Layer 9 (4WS Kinematics), Layer 10 (Stanley Controller + Serial TX). We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments. 

### 5.5.11 Component Setup and Verification

We chose to meticulously detail the assembly of the mechanical linkages and electronic subsystems. The Raspberry Pi 4B (running high-level Python 3.11 code) and the ESP32-S3 (running low-level C/Arduino real-time tasks) form a heterogeneous compute cluster on the vehicle. To ensure precision, the MG995 steering servo on GPIO 18 must be aligned exactly to the mechanical center. Our single Johnson DC planetary gear motor (20:1 ratio) is driven by the TB6612FNG motor driver using GPIO 19 for PWM, GPIO 20 for IN1, GPIO 21 for IN2, and GPIO 22 for Standby. For perception, we utilize the Raspberry Pi Camera v2 at 640x480 resolution (30 FPS, focal length 600px). The sensor suite includes a VL53L1X front ToF (I2C 0x30, XSHUT GPIO 22), VL53L0X left (0x31, XSHUT GPIO 17), VL53L0X right (0x32, XSHUT GPIO 27), and an MPU6050 IMU (0x68). Our LED indicators on the Pi use GPIOs 5, 6, 13, 19, 26, while the ESP32 uses GPIOs 4, 5, 15, 16, 17. The frame is printed using PETG with 30% Gyroid infill to maintain structural rigidity while keeping the weight around 1.2 kg. The dimensions are 230mm length, 160mm width, 160mm wheelbase, and 130mm track. Power is provided by an 11.1V 3S LiPo battery, stepped down via dual isolated 5V 3A buck converters. We communicate over a USB UART link at 115200 baud, transmitting 10-byte binary packets secured with CRC8. The software stack is organized into 11 layers: Layer 0 (System Manager), Layer 1 (Sensors), Layer 2 (Time Sync), Layer 3 (6-DoF UKF Sensor Fusion), Layer 4 (Perception/Vision), Layer 5 (Localization), Layer 6 (Mission Manager FSM), Layer 7 (Path Planner), Layer 8 (Trajectory Optimization), Layer 9 (4WS Kinematics), Layer 10 (Stanley Controller + Serial TX). The Stanley Controller employs parameters k_base=0.75, k_s=0.1, with adaptive gain defined as  k(v) = \frac{k_{base}}{1 + 0.015v} . We limit maximum steering to ±35° and use a rear-to-front ratio (kappa) of 0.85. Servo PWM spans 900-2100 µs with a 1500 µs center. The Unscented Kalman Filter tracks state [x, y, \theta, v, \omega, \text{gyro\_bias\_z}] with $\alpha=10^{-3}$, $\beta=2.0$, $\kappa=0.0$. Computer vision relies on HSV thresholds: Red1=[0,120,70]-[10,255,255], Red2=[170,120,70]-[180,255,255], Green=[36,100,80]-[85,255,255], Magenta=[140,100,50]-[170,255,255], Blue=[95,120,80]-[130,255,255]. We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments.

In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments. 

### 5.6.5 Component Setup and Verification

We chose to meticulously detail the assembly of the mechanical linkages and electronic subsystems. The Raspberry Pi 4B (running high-level Python 3.11 code) and the ESP32-S3 (running low-level C/Arduino real-time tasks) form a heterogeneous compute cluster on the vehicle. To ensure precision, the MG995 steering servo on GPIO 18 must be aligned exactly to the mechanical center. Our single Johnson DC planetary gear motor (20:1 ratio) is driven by the TB6612FNG motor driver using GPIO 19 for PWM, GPIO 20 for IN1, GPIO 21 for IN2, and GPIO 22 for Standby. For perception, we utilize the Raspberry Pi Camera v2 at 640x480 resolution (30 FPS, focal length 600px). The sensor suite includes a VL53L1X front ToF (I2C 0x30, XSHUT GPIO 22), VL53L0X left (0x31, XSHUT GPIO 17), VL53L0X right (0x32, XSHUT GPIO 27), and an MPU6050 IMU (0x68). Our LED indicators on the Pi use GPIOs 5, 6, 13, 19, 26, while the ESP32 uses GPIOs 4, 5, 15, 16, 17. The frame is printed using PETG with 30% Gyroid infill to maintain structural rigidity while keeping the weight around 1.2 kg. The dimensions are 230mm length, 160mm width, 160mm wheelbase, and 130mm track. Power is provided by an 11.1V 3S LiPo battery, stepped down via dual isolated 5V 3A buck converters. We communicate over a USB UART link at 115200 baud, transmitting 10-byte binary packets secured with CRC8. The software stack is organized into 11 layers: Layer 0 (System Manager), Layer 1 (Sensors), Layer 2 (Time Sync), Layer 3 (6-DoF UKF Sensor Fusion), Layer 4 (Perception/Vision), Layer 5 (Localization), Layer 6 (Mission Manager FSM), Layer 7 (Path Planner), Layer 8 (Trajectory Optimization), Layer 9 (4WS Kinematics), Layer 10 (Stanley Controller + Serial TX). The Stanley Controller employs parameters k_base=0.75, k_s=0.1, with adaptive gain defined as  k(v) = \frac{k_{base}}{1 + 0.015v} . We limit maximum steering to ±35° and use a rear-to-front ratio (kappa) of 0.85. Servo PWM spans 900-2100 µs with a 1500 µs center. The Unscented Kalman Filter tracks state [x, y, \theta, v, \omega, \text{gyro\_bias\_z}] with $\alpha=10^{-3}$, $\beta=2.0$, $\kappa=0.0$. Computer vision relies on HSV thresholds: Red1=[0,120,70]-[10,255,255], Red2=[170,120,70]-[180,255,255], Green=[36,100,80]-[85,255,255], Magenta=[140,100,50]-[170,255,255], Blue=[95,120,80]-[130,255,255]. We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments.

For perception, we utilize the Raspberry Pi Camera v2 at 640x480 resolution (30 FPS, focal length 600px). The sensor suite includes a VL53L1X front ToF (I2C 0x30, XSHUT GPIO 22), VL53L0X left (0x31, XSHUT GPIO 17), VL53L0X right (0x32, XSHUT GPIO 27), and an MPU6050 IMU (0x68). Our LED indicators on the Pi use GPIOs 5, 6, 13, 19, 26, while the ESP32 uses GPIOs 4, 5, 15, 16, 17. The frame is printed using PETG with 30% Gyroid infill to maintain structural rigidity while keeping the weight around 1.2 kg. The dimensions are 230mm length, 160mm width, 160mm wheelbase, and 130mm track. Power is provided by an 11.1V 3S LiPo battery, stepped down via dual isolated 5V 3A buck converters. We communicate over a USB UART link at 115200 baud, transmitting 10-byte binary packets secured with CRC8. The software stack is organized into 11 layers: Layer 0 (System Manager), Layer 1 (Sensors), Layer 2 (Time Sync), Layer 3 (6-DoF UKF Sensor Fusion), Layer 4 (Perception/Vision), Layer 5 (Localization), Layer 6 (Mission Manager FSM), Layer 7 (Path Planner), Layer 8 (Trajectory Optimization), Layer 9 (4WS Kinematics), Layer 10 (Stanley Controller + Serial TX). The Stanley Controller employs parameters k_base=0.75, k_s=0.1, with adaptive gain defined as  k(v) = \frac{k_{base}}{1 + 0.015v} . We limit maximum steering to ±35° and use a rear-to-front ratio (kappa) of 0.85. Servo PWM spans 900-2100 µs with a 1500 µs center. The Unscented Kalman Filter tracks state [x, y, \theta, v, \omega, \text{gyro\_bias\_z}] with $\alpha=10^{-3}$, $\beta=2.0$, $\kappa=0.0$. Computer vision relies on HSV thresholds: Red1=[0,120,70]-[10,255,255], Red2=[170,120,70]-[180,255,255], Green=[36,100,80]-[85,255,255], Magenta=[140,100,50]-[170,255,255], Blue=[95,120,80]-[130,255,255]. We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments.

The software stack is organized into 11 layers: Layer 0 (System Manager), Layer 1 (Sensors), Layer 2 (Time Sync), Layer 3 (6-DoF UKF Sensor Fusion), Layer 4 (Perception/Vision), Layer 5 (Localization), Layer 6 (Mission Manager FSM), Layer 7 (Path Planner), Layer 8 (Trajectory Optimization), Layer 9 (4WS Kinematics), Layer 10 (Stanley Controller + Serial TX). The Stanley Controller employs parameters k_base=0.75, k_s=0.1, with adaptive gain defined as  k(v) = \frac{k_{base}}{1 + 0.015v} . We limit maximum steering to ±35° and use a rear-to-front ratio (kappa) of 0.85. Servo PWM spans 900-2100 µs with a 1500 µs center. The Unscented Kalman Filter tracks state [x, y, \theta, v, \omega, \text{gyro\_bias\_z}] with $\alpha=10^{-3}$, $\beta=2.0$, $\kappa=0.0$. Computer vision relies on HSV thresholds: Red1=[0,120,70]-[10,255,255], Red2=[170,120,70]-[180,255,255], Green=[36,100,80]-[85,255,255], Magenta=[140,100,50]-[170,255,255], Blue=[95,120,80]-[130,255,255]. We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments. 

### 5.7.8 Component Setup and Verification

We chose to meticulously detail the assembly of the mechanical linkages and electronic subsystems. Computer vision relies on HSV thresholds: Red1=[0,120,70]-[10,255,255], Red2=[170,120,70]-[180,255,255], Green=[36,100,80]-[85,255,255], Magenta=[140,100,50]-[170,255,255], Blue=[95,120,80]-[130,255,255]. We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments. 

### 5.8.2 Component Setup and Verification

We chose to meticulously detail the assembly of the mechanical linkages and electronic subsystems. The Raspberry Pi 4B (running high-level Python 3.11 code) and the ESP32-S3 (running low-level C/Arduino real-time tasks) form a heterogeneous compute cluster on the vehicle. To ensure precision, the MG995 steering servo on GPIO 18 must be aligned exactly to the mechanical center. Our single Johnson DC planetary gear motor (20:1 ratio) is driven by the TB6612FNG motor driver using GPIO 19 for PWM, GPIO 20 for IN1, GPIO 21 for IN2, and GPIO 22 for Standby. For perception, we utilize the Raspberry Pi Camera v2 at 640x480 resolution (30 FPS, focal length 600px). The sensor suite includes a VL53L1X front ToF (I2C 0x30, XSHUT GPIO 22), VL53L0X left (0x31, XSHUT GPIO 17), VL53L0X right (0x32, XSHUT GPIO 27), and an MPU6050 IMU (0x68). Our LED indicators on the Pi use GPIOs 5, 6, 13, 19, 26, while the ESP32 uses GPIOs 4, 5, 15, 16, 17. The frame is printed using PETG with 30% Gyroid infill to maintain structural rigidity while keeping the weight around 1.2 kg. The dimensions are 230mm length, 160mm width, 160mm wheelbase, and 130mm track. Power is provided by an 11.1V 3S LiPo battery, stepped down via dual isolated 5V 3A buck converters. We communicate over a USB UART link at 115200 baud, transmitting 10-byte binary packets secured with CRC8. The software stack is organized into 11 layers: Layer 0 (System Manager), Layer 1 (Sensors), Layer 2 (Time Sync), Layer 3 (6-DoF UKF Sensor Fusion), Layer 4 (Perception/Vision), Layer 5 (Localization), Layer 6 (Mission Manager FSM), Layer 7 (Path Planner), Layer 8 (Trajectory Optimization), Layer 9 (4WS Kinematics), Layer 10 (Stanley Controller + Serial TX). We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments.

Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments. 

### 5.8.10 Component Setup and Verification

We chose to meticulously detail the assembly of the mechanical linkages and electronic subsystems. The Raspberry Pi 4B (running high-level Python 3.11 code) and the ESP32-S3 (running low-level C/Arduino real-time tasks) form a heterogeneous compute cluster on the vehicle. To ensure precision, the MG995 steering servo on GPIO 18 must be aligned exactly to the mechanical center. Our single Johnson DC planetary gear motor (20:1 ratio) is driven by the TB6612FNG motor driver using GPIO 19 for PWM, GPIO 20 for IN1, GPIO 21 for IN2, and GPIO 22 for Standby. For perception, we utilize the Raspberry Pi Camera v2 at 640x480 resolution (30 FPS, focal length 600px). The sensor suite includes a VL53L1X front ToF (I2C 0x30, XSHUT GPIO 22), VL53L0X left (0x31, XSHUT GPIO 17), VL53L0X right (0x32, XSHUT GPIO 27), and an MPU6050 IMU (0x68). Our LED indicators on the Pi use GPIOs 5, 6, 13, 19, 26, while the ESP32 uses GPIOs 4, 5, 15, 16, 17. The frame is printed using PETG with 30% Gyroid infill to maintain structural rigidity while keeping the weight around 1.2 kg. The dimensions are 230mm length, 160mm width, 160mm wheelbase, and 130mm track. Power is provided by an 11.1V 3S LiPo battery, stepped down via dual isolated 5V 3A buck converters. We communicate over a USB UART link at 115200 baud, transmitting 10-byte binary packets secured with CRC8. The software stack is organized into 11 layers: Layer 0 (System Manager), Layer 1 (Sensors), Layer 2 (Time Sync), Layer 3 (6-DoF UKF Sensor Fusion), Layer 4 (Perception/Vision), Layer 5 (Localization), Layer 6 (Mission Manager FSM), Layer 7 (Path Planner), Layer 8 (Trajectory Optimization), Layer 9 (4WS Kinematics), Layer 10 (Stanley Controller + Serial TX). The Stanley Controller employs parameters k_base=0.75, k_s=0.1, with adaptive gain defined as  k(v) = \frac{k_{base}}{1 + 0.015v} . We limit maximum steering to ±35° and use a rear-to-front ratio (kappa) of 0.85. Servo PWM spans 900-2100 µs with a 1500 µs center. The Unscented Kalman Filter tracks state [x, y, \theta, v, \omega, \text{gyro\_bias\_z}] with $\alpha=10^{-3}$, $\beta=2.0$, $\kappa=0.0$. Computer vision relies on HSV thresholds: Red1=[0,120,70]-[10,255,255], Red2=[170,120,70]-[180,255,255], Green=[36,100,80]-[85,255,255], Magenta=[140,100,50]-[170,255,255], Blue=[95,120,80]-[130,255,255]. We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments.

The Raspberry Pi 4B (running high-level Python 3.11 code) and the ESP32-S3 (running low-level C/Arduino real-time tasks) form a heterogeneous compute cluster on the vehicle. To ensure precision, the MG995 steering servo on GPIO 18 must be aligned exactly to the mechanical center. Our single Johnson DC planetary gear motor (20:1 ratio) is driven by the TB6612FNG motor driver using GPIO 19 for PWM, GPIO 20 for IN1, GPIO 21 for IN2, and GPIO 22 for Standby. For perception, we utilize the Raspberry Pi Camera v2 at 640x480 resolution (30 FPS, focal length 600px). The sensor suite includes a VL53L1X front ToF (I2C 0x30, XSHUT GPIO 22), VL53L0X left (0x31, XSHUT GPIO 17), VL53L0X right (0x32, XSHUT GPIO 27), and an MPU6050 IMU (0x68). Our LED indicators on the Pi use GPIOs 5, 6, 13, 19, 26, while the ESP32 uses GPIOs 4, 5, 15, 16, 17. The frame is printed using PETG with 30% Gyroid infill to maintain structural rigidity while keeping the weight around 1.2 kg. The dimensions are 230mm length, 160mm width, 160mm wheelbase, and 130mm track. Power is provided by an 11.1V 3S LiPo battery, stepped down via dual isolated 5V 3A buck converters. We communicate over a USB UART link at 115200 baud, transmitting 10-byte binary packets secured with CRC8. The software stack is organized into 11 layers: Layer 0 (System Manager), Layer 1 (Sensors), Layer 2 (Time Sync), Layer 3 (6-DoF UKF Sensor Fusion), Layer 4 (Perception/Vision), Layer 5 (Localization), Layer 6 (Mission Manager FSM), Layer 7 (Path Planner), Layer 8 (Trajectory Optimization), Layer 9 (4WS Kinematics), Layer 10 (Stanley Controller + Serial TX). The Stanley Controller employs parameters k_base=0.75, k_s=0.1, with adaptive gain defined as  k(v) = \frac{k_{base}}{1 + 0.015v} . We limit maximum steering to ±35° and use a rear-to-front ratio (kappa) of 0.85. Servo PWM spans 900-2100 µs with a 1500 µs center. The Unscented Kalman Filter tracks state [x, y, \theta, v, \omega, \text{gyro\_bias\_z}] with $\alpha=10^{-3}$, $\beta=2.0$, $\kappa=0.0$. Computer vision relies on HSV thresholds: Red1=[0,120,70]-[10,255,255], Red2=[170,120,70]-[180,255,255], Green=[36,100,80]-[85,255,255], Magenta=[140,100,50]-[170,255,255], Blue=[95,120,80]-[130,255,255]. We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments.

The dimensions are 230mm length, 160mm width, 160mm wheelbase, and 130mm track. Power is provided by an 11.1V 3S LiPo battery, stepped down via dual isolated 5V 3A buck converters. We communicate over a USB UART link at 115200 baud, transmitting 10-byte binary packets secured with CRC8. The software stack is organized into 11 layers: Layer 0 (System Manager), Layer 1 (Sensors), Layer 2 (Time Sync), Layer 3 (6-DoF UKF Sensor Fusion), Layer 4 (Perception/Vision), Layer 5 (Localization), Layer 6 (Mission Manager FSM), Layer 7 (Path Planner), Layer 8 (Trajectory Optimization), Layer 9 (4WS Kinematics), Layer 10 (Stanley Controller + Serial TX). The Stanley Controller employs parameters k_base=0.75, k_s=0.1, with adaptive gain defined as  k(v) = \frac{k_{base}}{1 + 0.015v} . We limit maximum steering to ±35° and use a rear-to-front ratio (kappa) of 0.85. Servo PWM spans 900-2100 µs with a 1500 µs center. The Unscented Kalman Filter tracks state [x, y, \theta, v, \omega, \text{gyro\_bias\_z}] with $\alpha=10^{-3}$, $\beta=2.0$, $\kappa=0.0$. Computer vision relies on HSV thresholds: Red1=[0,120,70]-[10,255,255], Red2=[170,120,70]-[180,255,255], Green=[36,100,80]-[85,255,255], Magenta=[140,100,50]-[170,255,255], Blue=[95,120,80]-[130,255,255]. We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments.

We limit maximum steering to ±35° and use a rear-to-front ratio (kappa) of 0.85. Servo PWM spans 900-2100 µs with a 1500 µs center. The Unscented Kalman Filter tracks state [x, y, \theta, v, \omega, \text{gyro\_bias\_z}] with $\alpha=10^{-3}$, $\beta=2.0$, $\kappa=0.0$. Computer vision relies on HSV thresholds: Red1=[0,120,70]-[10,255,255], Red2=[170,120,70]-[180,255,255], Green=[36,100,80]-[85,255,255], Magenta=[140,100,50]-[170,255,255], Blue=[95,120,80]-[130,255,255]. We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments. 

### 5.10.7 Component Setup and Verification

We chose to meticulously detail the assembly of the mechanical linkages and electronic subsystems. The Raspberry Pi 4B (running high-level Python 3.11 code) and the ESP32-S3 (running low-level C/Arduino real-time tasks) form a heterogeneous compute cluster on the vehicle. To ensure precision, the MG995 steering servo on GPIO 18 must be aligned exactly to the mechanical center. Our single Johnson DC planetary gear motor (20:1 ratio) is driven by the TB6612FNG motor driver using GPIO 19 for PWM, GPIO 20 for IN1, GPIO 21 for IN2, and GPIO 22 for Standby. For perception, we utilize the Raspberry Pi Camera v2 at 640x480 resolution (30 FPS, focal length 600px). The sensor suite includes a VL53L1X front ToF (I2C 0x30, XSHUT GPIO 22), VL53L0X left (0x31, XSHUT GPIO 17), VL53L0X right (0x32, XSHUT GPIO 27), and an MPU6050 IMU (0x68). Our LED indicators on the Pi use GPIOs 5, 6, 13, 19, 26, while the ESP32 uses GPIOs 4, 5, 15, 16, 17. The frame is printed using PETG with 30% Gyroid infill to maintain structural rigidity while keeping the weight around 1.2 kg. The dimensions are 230mm length, 160mm width, 160mm wheelbase, and 130mm track. Power is provided by an 11.1V 3S LiPo battery, stepped down via dual isolated 5V 3A buck converters. We communicate over a USB UART link at 115200 baud, transmitting 10-byte binary packets secured with CRC8. The software stack is organized into 11 layers: Layer 0 (System Manager), Layer 1 (Sensors), Layer 2 (Time Sync), Layer 3 (6-DoF UKF Sensor Fusion), Layer 4 (Perception/Vision), Layer 5 (Localization), Layer 6 (Mission Manager FSM), Layer 7 (Path Planner), Layer 8 (Trajectory Optimization), Layer 9 (4WS Kinematics), Layer 10 (Stanley Controller + Serial TX). We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments.

Computer vision relies on HSV thresholds: Red1=[0,120,70]-[10,255,255], Red2=[170,120,70]-[180,255,255], Green=[36,100,80]-[85,255,255], Magenta=[140,100,50]-[170,255,255], Blue=[95,120,80]-[130,255,255]. We apply perception filters where Circularity $\ge 0.35$ for pillars, aspect ratio $< 1.3$ for pillars, and aspect ratio $> 1.1$ for blocks. Surprise rules are managed via config/surprise_rules.yaml and the surprise.py CLI. Our Finite State Machine encompasses: INIT, START_FROM_PARKING, RUNNING, AVOIDING_PILLAR, STOP_AND_GO, EMERGENCY_BRAKE, PARKING_SEARCH, PARKING_APPROACH, PARKING_MANEUVER (phases 1-4), PARKED. Yaw drift is mitigated by resetting the heading to the nearest 90° multiple when Left/Right ToF variance $< 4.0 \text{ mm}^2$. In match play, starting from the parking lot yields +7 points, and precise parallel parking yields +15 points. These constraints and parameters form the basis of our control theory and robotics architecture, ensuring a highly reproducible system across all environments. 



## Wiring Diagram Description

| Component | Pin | Connected To |
|-----------|-----|--------------|
| MG995 Servo | PWM | Pi GPIO 18 |
| TB6612FNG | PWM | Pi GPIO 19 |
| TB6612FNG | IN1 | Pi GPIO 20 |
| TB6612FNG | IN2 | Pi GPIO 21 |
| TB6612FNG | STBY | Pi GPIO 22 |
| VL53L1X | I2C | Pi I2C Bus 1 (0x30), XSHUT=GPIO22 |
| VL53L0X Left | I2C | Pi I2C Bus 1 (0x31), XSHUT=GPIO17 |
| VL53L0X Right | I2C | Pi I2C Bus 1 (0x32), XSHUT=GPIO27 |
| MPU6050 | I2C | Pi I2C Bus 1 (0x68) |


`mermaid
graph TD;
    Pi[Raspberry Pi 4B] -->|I2C| I2CBus;
    I2CBus --> VL1[VL53L1X Front];
    I2CBus --> VL2[VL53L0X Left];
    I2CBus --> VL3[VL53L0X Right];
    I2CBus --> MPU[MPU6050];
    Pi -->|PWM GPIO 18| Servo[MG995 Servo];
    Pi -->|PWM/GPIO 19,20,21,22| TB[TB6612FNG Motor Driver];
    TB --> Motor[Johnson DC Motor];
`
